# PREFACE
## BY THE AUTHOR.

How indispensable the prayer of faith is, in order to obtain all kinds of spiritual and bodily blessings from above, has been sufficiently shown by the Apostle James in Chapter 1, verses 5, 6, 7, and 8. <note>original: "C. 1. v. 5. 6. 7. 8." The author refers to the Epistle of James, which warns that a person who doubts is like a wave of the sea and should not expect to receive anything from the Lord.</note> In these verses, he particularly demonstrates the impossibility of receiving anything from God by examining the nature and characteristics of unbelief. For, he says,

<summary>This page opens the author's formal preface, establishing the "prayer of faith" as the essential requirement for receiving divine favor. Citing the Apostle James, the author argues that the nature of unbelief inherently prevents a person from receiving blessings from God.</summary>
<keywords>prayer of faith, Apostle James, unbelief, spiritual blessings, divine grace, preface, theology</keywords>

# PREFACE.

he, <note>This continues the quotation from the Epistle of James: "For he that wavereth is like a wave of the sea driven with the wind and tossed."</note> he who doubts is just like the waves of the sea, which are driven and tossed by the wind. Let such a person not think that he will receive anything from the Lord. A doubter is unstable in all his ways. Just as it is arranged in nature, that during impetuous sea waves roaring back and forth and during stormy winds, the benevolent influences of heaven can be greatly hindered, and its <term>astral fire</term> <note>In early modern natural philosophy and alchemy, "astral fire" refers to a celestial, life-giving energy believed to radiate from the stars into the physical world.</note> and light-force cannot so easily instill themselves; likewise, the creatures that live in the sea, such as the **pearl mussels**, can never better open themselves to the reception of the trickling **heavenly dew** <note>It was a common historical belief that pearls were formed when mussels rose to the surface of the water to catch the morning dew, which then solidified into gems.</note> than when the sea is still and the atmosphere is clear

## The First Hour of Rest in God.

->*At the breaking of the lovely dawn.*<-

->Psalm 63, 7.<- <note>In many English Bibles, this corresponds to Psalm 63:6: "When I remember thee upon my bed, and meditate on thee in the night watches." The author's version emphasizes speaking or communing with God upon waking.</note>

> *When I awake, I speak of you.*

O! You infinite ocean of origin <note>original: "Ursprungsmeer." A poetic image common in German mysticism, depicting God as the source or "sea" from which all life flows and to which it returns.</note> of all created things, you power of all powers, and spirit of all spirits, you nourisher and sustainer of all things; because from you light, life, and every good thing proceed, so shall my heart be directed toward you this day, to glorify your great name and to proclaim your honor. Your wondrous goodness has also become great toward me today, in that you allow me to behold your sunlight anew with such grace. Oh, how many during this night [have met] their na- <meta>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "tür-" on the following page suggests the word is "natürlichen Tod," or "natural death."</meta>

<vocab>Hour of rest, dawn, Psalm, ocean of origin, wondrous goodness, sunlight</vocab>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a daily devotional practice, offering a morning prayer that praises God as the infinite source of all life and light. The speaker expresses gratitude for surviving the night and being allowed to see the dawn, contrasting their life with those who may have died during the night.</summary>
<keywords>morning prayer, divine origin, gratitude, dawn, creation, providence</keywords>

-tural sleep with the sleep of death! <note>The author employs a common Baroque-era trope, comparing the state of sleep to death—suggesting that every morning is a small "resurrection" provided by God's grace.</note> And how many, perhaps, will have sunk thereby into the terrible darkness of a hellish night of death, into a black, frightening, and despairing eternity, wherein the light never shines for them! Your merciful love toward me is, therefore, to be esteemed as precious and held in high regard. For as I now awake again from my sleep, it is as much to me as if you had awakened me from the dark night of the grave. I—yes, I must certainly imagine—as if you, as it were, every morning when you grant me life anew, had also created me anew, just as you [drew] me out of the deep night of a dar- <meta>catchword: -kness (from the German "dunkeln")</meta>

With the first sight of this great world-empire, his free spirit saw itself set as lord and king over all these earthly splendors. All the fruits of the outer world stood prepared for his enjoyment. But nothing within it was so pleasant that it should have captured this free spirit and drawn it away from the love of God. Rather, he delighted <note>original: "weidete er sich," literally "pastured himself," a common Pietist metaphor for the soul finding nourishment or deep satisfaction.</note> himself solely in the inexpressible love of God, and ruled over the outer realm of nature.

## Psalm 104.

### A Song of Praise to God from the Book of Nature. <note>The "Book of Nature" refers to the theological idea that God’s existence and attributes can be understood by observing the physical universe, acting as a secondary "scripture" alongside the Bible.</note>

Bless the Lord, O my soul!

O Lord my God, you are very magnificent; you are beautiful and mightily adorned.

Light is the garment you wear; you stretch out the heavens like a curtain <note>original: "Teppich," which can mean carpet, tapestry, or hanging cloth.</note>.

You lay the beams of your chambers in the waters above; you ride upon the clouds as if on a chariot, and walk upon the wings of the wind.

You who make your angels into winds <note>The German "Winde" mirrors the dual meaning of the original biblical languages, where the same word can mean "wind," "breath," or "spirit."</note>, and your ministers into flames of fire.

You who established the earth upon its foundations, so that it remains forever and ever.

# A Prayer,

## *for a well-ordered life of prayer and work.*

---

->Psalm 104, 20. 21. 22. 23.<-

> *You bring darkness, so that it becomes night, then*
> *all the wild animals stir; the young li-*
> *ons, who roar for their prey, and*
> *seek their food from God. But when*
> *the sun rises, they withdraw from there,*
> *and lie down in their dens. Thus*
> *man goes forth to his work, and to*
> *his labor <note>original: "Ackerwerk," specifically referring to field work or agriculture.</note> until the evening.*

Most gracious Creator and Lord of my life, we must accuse ourselves before you, because we have brought our whole life into a wretched disorder, have departed from your love with our free spirit <note>original: "freyen Geist," referring here to the human capacity for choice or the human soul.</note>, and have presumed to use creation <note>original: "Gebrauch der Kreatur," referring to the way humans interact with and utilize the created world.</note> according to our own self-will <note>original: "Eigenwilligkeit."</note>.

Prayer, Psalm 104, Creator, Disorder, Self-will, Work, Field labor

# Psalm 111.

## Thanksgiving for God’s Physical and Spiritual Blessing.

I thank the Lord with my whole heart, in the council of the upright <term>Rath der Frommen: a gathering of the faithful or a private circle of the godly</term>, and in the congregation.

Great are the works of the Lord; whoever considers them has pure delight in them.

What he ordains is praiseworthy and glorious, and his righteousness remains eternally.

He has established a memorial of his wonders, the gracious and merciful God.

He gives food <note>This reinforces the theme from the previous page: that God provides "bread" for both the body and the soul</note> to those who fear him; he remembers his covenant forever.

He lets his mighty deeds be proclaimed to his people, that he might give them the inheritance of the nations <note>original: "Heyden"; historically translated as "heathens" or "gentiles," referring here to the lands of other nations promised to the people of God</note>.

## The 16th Psalm.

### *A thanksgiving for the promised and hoped-for rescue from death.*

Preserve me, O God, for I trust in You.

I have said to the Lord: You are indeed the Lord; I must suffer for Your sake. <note>This phrasing reflects a specific devotional interpretation; most standard translations of Psalm 16:2 read "I have no good apart from You." The focus here on suffering "for Your sake" emphasizes the believer's identification with Christ's trials.</note>

For the saints that are on earth, and for the noble ones, in whom I have all my delight. <note>The "noble ones" (Herrlichen) refers to those who are spiritually distinguished or virtuous in the eyes of God.</note>

But those who hasten after another <note>This refers to those who pursue other gods or idols.</note> shall have great heartache.

I will not offer their drink offerings of blood, nor take their names upon my lips. <term>Drink offering (Trankopfer): A ritual pouring out of wine or other liquids as a sacrifice. In this context, it refers to rejecting the pagan or unholy rituals of those who do not follow God.</term>

**Vocabulary**

| German | English Translation |
|---|---|
| Psalm 16 | Psalm 16 |
| Dankſagung | Thanksgiving |
| Errettung | Rescue / Salvation |
| Heiligen | Saints |
| Trankopfer | Drink offering |
| Herzenleid | Heartache / Sorrow |

<summary>This page presents the opening verses of Psalm 16, framed as a prayer of gratitude for God's protection against death. It emphasizes the speaker's loyalty to God and the community of believers while rejecting the practices of the unfaithful.</summary>
<keywords>Psalm 16, thanksgiving, rescue from death, saints, idolatry, trust in God, devotion</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Psalm 16, picking up at verse 5. The previous page established this as a prayer of rescue from death and a declaration of loyalty to God. The text transitions here from rejecting idols to celebrating the spiritual inheritance found in the Lord.</meta>

The Lord, however, is my good and my portion; You maintain my inheritance.

The lot has fallen to me in the loveliest way; a beautiful inheritance has become mine. <note>The "lot" refers to the ancient practice of dividing land by casting lots; the speaker feels their "portion" or spiritual territory assigned by God is perfect.</note>

I praise the Lord, who has advised me; my kidneys also chasten me in the night. <note>original: "Nieren". In biblical language, the kidneys (reins) were considered the seat of the emotions and conscience. The "chastening" suggests a period of nightly soul-searching and divine instruction.</note>

I have the Lord always before my eyes; for He is at my right hand, therefore I shall remain secure.

Therefore my heart rejoices, and my honor is glad; my flesh also will rest in safety. <note>The word "honor" (Ehre) here is a traditional translation of a Hebrew term referring to one's inner being, soul, or tongue.</note>

For You will not leave my soul in Hell; and will not allow Your Holy One to see decay. <note>original: "Hölle". In this context, it refers to Sheol or the grave. For the early modern reader, this verse was a crucial prophecy of the Resurrection of Christ.</note>

You make known to me the path to life; before You is fullness of joy, and lovely being at Your right hand forevermore.

# Daily Hourly Prayers.

->*In various devotions to pour out the heart before God hourly, to tear oneself away from the world, and to direct one’s conduct toward heaven.*<-

---

## The First Devotion.

### Concerning the Knowledge of God, and specifically, that God is the Light and Life of Mankind.

# The Thirteenth Devotion.

## For True Humility of Heart Toward One’s Neighbor.

->Revelation 18:6.<-

> Render to her even as she rendered unto you. <note>original: "Bezahlet sie, wie sie euch bezahlet hat." This verse from the Book of Revelation describes the principle of divine justice where actions are returned upon the doer.</note>

Righteous and holy God, you who repay everyone according to their works, and pay back upon their own head <term>upon their own head: "auf seinen Kopf." A biblical idiom (similar to Psalm 7:16) suggesting that the consequences of a person's actions return to affect them directly.</term> how they have acted toward you and their neighbor. Oh, let me always keep this before my eyes for the true humbling of my heart, so that in true lowliness of spirit and with gentleness I may gladly bear my brother’s faults, so that I may speak to you with the same confidence in your mercy: Forgive us our debts, as we forgive our debtors. <note>A reference to the petition in the Lord's Prayer (Matthew 6:12), which links human forgiveness of others to divine forgiveness from God.</note>

### *For Gracious Deliverance from our Sinful Misery.*

->*For thine is the kingdom.*<- <note>The author is reflecting on the concluding portion of the Lord's Prayer, often called the Doxology, which acknowledges God’s supreme authority.</note>

## The Second Reflection

### *Concerning the Restoration of the Corrupted Creature, namely under the Economy of the Father, according to the First Principle of Fire.*

->John 6:44<-

> No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him.

Dearest Father! Everything that You have undertaken with us poor humans from the beginning of the world, and even that which appears to have happened in Your wrath, is nonetheless in the end pure love, and is determined toward the <term>restoration<note>original: "Wiederbringung" — a reference to the doctrine of *Apokatastasis* or the restoration of all things to their original divine state, a central theme in radical Pietism.</note></term> of our salvation. For though we were indeed not so swift with the desire of our will to [turn away] from the <term>light-essence<note>original: "Lichtwesen" — in theosophical terms, this refers to the spiritual, unfallen substance of the soul or the divine nature.</note></term> of Your—
